\section{Abstract}\label{abstract}

This note develops survey-weighted double-score matching (WDSM) for probability samples in which each sampled unit carries a survey weight and matching is performed on a propensity score and a prognostic score. The target estimands are the population average treatment effect (PATE) and the population average treatment effect on the treated (PATT) under two sampling regimes: retrospective sampling, in which sample inclusion may depend on treatment status, and prospective sampling, in which sampling occurs before treatment assignment. The paper works from the exact estimator outward and gives one synchronized four-cell theorem package.

The central structural correction is that the exact survey-weighted imputation rule is within-match survey-weight-normalized. For a focal unit \(i\) and matched unit \(j\in\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)\), the correct within-match coefficient is
\[
\lambda_{i\to j}^{\mathrm{ex}}
=
\frac{\text{survey weight of matched unit }j}{\text{sum of survey weights over the matched set for focal unit }i},
\]
not the equal-share proxy \(1/M\). Accordingly, the correct reuse object is the exact survey-weighted matching frequency induced jointly by the outer weight of the focal unit and the within-match survey-weight-normalized inner share. The known-score theory is therefore rebuilt on the exact-share scale: exact survey-weighted matching frequencies, exact matching-frequency rewrites, exact decompositions, exact conditional residual variances, and centered known-score limits are all derived for that estimator rather than for an equal-share surrogate.

The theorem stack follows the same assumption discipline as the core matching papers used as benchmarks: design and identification assumptions and matching-space regularity are kept primitive, while the hard fixed-\(M\) matching algebra is pushed into lemmas.

\textbf{Important boundary statement.} No main-theorem argument below assumes that the retrospective arm-specific survey weights \(w_z(\mathbf X)\) or the prospective survey weight \(w(\mathbf X)\) are measurable or smooth functions of the double score alone. In particular, the main exact-share theory does \emph{not} assume a bridge of the form \(w(\mathbf X)=\omega(\mathbf D(\mathbf X))\), and therefore does \emph{not} force the sampling mechanism to be score-sufficient. Any stronger score-smooth matched-unit-weight statement appears only inside optional sufficient closure routes stated after the main exact-share theory has already been laid out.

In particular, the two-dimensional score-radius rate, the exact discrepancy bound, the matching-frequency rewrites, the heterogeneity Hájek terms, and the martingale-array residual limits are derived rather than imposed as theorem-level assumptions. In the known-score layer, the remaining explicit closure conditions are the exact-share moment-map convergence condition and the cell-specific coefficient-square stabilization conditions. Proposition~4.8C records a weaker local conditional matched-side survey-weight-law route for the exact-share moment-map closure, Propositions~5.4A, 6.4A, 7.5A, and~8.5A together with Appendix~P record one optional equal-share validation subclass for the residual-variance closures, and Corollaries~5.6B, 6.6B, 7.7B, and~8.7B collect the corresponding benchmark-style primitive sufficient CLTs scenario by scenario. The main exact-share scenario theorems do not impose either optional route as a primitive framework assumption. In the estimated-score layer, the paper does not assume a smooth plug-in expansion of the matching functional. Instead it records the fixed-law and changing-law variance formulas through the non-smooth local-experiment / Le Cam route on the score-normalized likelihood scale used by Abadie--Imbens (2016) and Yang--Zhang (2022). Under fixed law, the second adjustment block is zero in all four cells; under a genuine changing-law experiment, the additional term is
\[
\gamma_{A,2}^{\top}\Sigma_{A,\theta}\gamma_{A,2}.
\]
Section~10.1B further isolates the ordinary survey-weighted M-estimation part of the first step, so the genuinely matching-specific frontier is narrowed to the local-experiment coupling and discretization steps. To keep the benchmark-paper assumption boundary explicit, the remaining nonprimitive pieces are grouped explicitly below as Closure Input~C1, Closure Input~C2, and Closure Input~E2 rather than being presented as primitive framework assumptions.


\section{Notation alignment with the current manuscript draft}\label{notation-alignment-with-the-current-manuscript-draft}

This version is aligned to the notation standard used in \texttt{WDSM\_main\_0406.tex}. At the unit level we write
\[
\{Y_i(0),Y_i(1),Z_i,S_i,\mathbf X_i\}_{i=1}^n.
\]
For generic probability statements and conditional expectations we write \(X\) for a generic covariate vector and \(Y(z)\) for a generic potential outcome. The propensity score is \(e(X)=P(Z=1\mid X)\), the arm-specific prognostic score is \(\Psi_z(X)=E[Y(z)\mid X]\), and the treatment-specific double score is
\[
\mathbf D_z(X)=(e(X),\Psi_z(X)), \qquad z\in\{0,1\}.
\]
The population estimands are
\[
\tau_{\mathrm{PATE}}=E_{\Omega}[Y(1)-Y(0)],
\qquad
\tau_{\mathrm{PATT}}=E_{\Omega}[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid Z=1].
\]
The PATT theorems are written on the weaker one-sided ATT/PATT route unless a stronger shortcut is explicitly invoked. Accordingly,
\[
\mu_{1\mid 1}(X)=E_{\Omega}[Y(1)\mid X,Z=1],
\qquad
\tau_T(X)=\mu_{1\mid 1}(X)-\mu_0(X),
\qquad
\sigma_{1\mid 1}^2(X)=\operatorname{Var}_{\Omega}(Y(1)\mid X,Z=1).
\]
The shortcut \((\mu_{1\mid1},\tau_T,\sigma_{1\mid1}^2)\to(\mu_1,\tau,\sigma_1^2)\) is used only when full treatment ignorability is explicitly assumed. In the displayed derivations we avoid informal donor/recipient terminology. Unit \(i\) is the \emph{index unit}; \(\mathcal J_z(i)\) is the opposite-arm matched set used to impute \(Y_i(z)\); \(\lambda_{i\to j}\) is the within-match coefficient; and \(K\) denotes the corresponding survey-weighted matching frequency or reuse frequency.

\section{0. Assumption discipline relative to the benchmark matching papers}\label{assumption-discipline-blueprint-relative-to-the-source-papers}

The guiding rule of the present rewrite is the same assumption
discipline used in Abadie--Imbens (2016), Yang--Zhang (2022), and
Chen--Han (2024): keep identification and basic regularity primitive,
and move the hard fixed-\(M\) matching geometry and reuse algebra into
lemmas. The theorem stack below is therefore organized in four layers.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\tightlist
\item
  \textbf{Primitive design and identification layer.} This is the level
  of unconfoundedness / one-sided ignorability, overlap,
  probability-sampling recovery identities, and the distinction between
  retrospective and prospective sampling. These are the causal and
  sampling assumptions.
\item
  \textbf{Primitive matching-space regularity layer.} This is the level
  of compact support, density bounded away from zero and infinity,
  Lipschitz conditional means, and \(2+\delta\) moments on the chosen
  two-dimensional score space. This matches the role played by the
  regularity blocks in Abadie--Imbens, Yang--Zhang, and Chen--Han.
\item
  \textbf{Derived geometric and algebraic layer.} Once the first two
  layers hold, the score-radius rate, exact within-match survey-weight-normalized rewrite,
  exact bias decomposition, focal-unit-minus-matched-set discrepancy order,
  heterogeneity Hájek CLT, and exact conditional residual variance
  formulas are derived as lemmas. They are not promoted to theorem-level
  assumptions.
\item
  \textbf{Estimated-score local-experiment layer.} For estimated scores,
  the present note follows the Abadie--Imbens / Yang--Zhang route: LAN /
  Le Cam plus a non-smooth perturbation statement. The note does not
  replace that route by an unproved direct smooth expansion of the
  estimated-score matching estimator.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Important negative statement on the survey-weight assumptions.} No main-theorem argument below assumes that the retrospective arm-specific survey weights $w_z(\mathbf X)$ or the prospective survey weight $w(\mathbf X)$ can be written as a measurable or smooth function of the double score alone. In particular, the main exact-share theory does \emph{not} impose a condition of the form $w(\mathbf X)=\omega(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X))$ or $w(\mathbf X)=\omega(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X))$, and it therefore does \emph{not} force the sampling mechanism to be score-sufficient. It also does not, by itself, assert $S\perp Z\mid \mathbf D(\mathbf X)$ or $(Y(0),Y(1),Z)\perp S\mid \mathbf D(\mathbf X)$. Any score-smooth matched-unit-weight restriction appears only inside optional sufficient closure propositions used to validate special subclasses \emph{after} the main exact-share four-cell theory has already been stated.

Accordingly, the present version does \textbf{not} assume as primitives
any of the following theorem ingredients: the \(n^{-1/2}\) average
score-radius rate in dimension \(2\), the exact matching-frequency
decomposition, the exact survey-weighted matching-frequency residual variance formula, the
focal-unit-minus-matched-set discrepancy order, or the Hájek CLTs for the
heterogeneity blocks. Those pieces are proved below. The remaining
explicit known-score-layer closure conditions are the exact-share moment-map
closure block and the coefficient-square stabilization step for the
exact survey-weighted matching-frequency residual array. The optional collapse from
exact shares to equal shares is also not part of the main theorem path;
when it is wanted, it is derived from the separate local within-set weight
homogeneity route in Corollary 4.8A together with Lemma 4.8B.

A second improvement in this detailed version is proof transparency.
the expanded in-place known-score proofs rewrites the four known-score centered-CLT proofs in the requested
chain-of-equation style and makes the final heterogeneity-plus-residual
closure explicit through the stable martingale refinement recorded in
Imported Lemma~4.2B.


\subsection{0A. Explicit crosswalk to the benchmark-paper assumption level}\label{explicit-crosswalk-to-the-benchmark-paper-assumption-level}

The benchmark papers used throughout the appendix separate \emph{primitive framework assumptions} from \emph{derived fixed-\(M\) matching lemmas}. The present note now follows that same hierarchy explicitly.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  In Abadie--Imbens (2006), the theorem layer is the primitive support,
  overlap, smoothness, and moment block. The matching discrepancy,
  bounded moments of reuse counts, bias order, centered CLT, and
  bias-corrected theorem are then derived as Lemma~1, Lemma~2,
  Lemma~3, Theorems~1--8.
\item
  In the Abadie--Imbens (2016) supplement, the estimated-score layer is
  not obtained by differentiating the matching operator as though it
  were smooth. Instead the paper adds preliminary lemmas, a local
  experiment, a reverse likelihood-ratio expansion, Le Cam's third
  lemma, and a discretization step.
\item
  In Yang--Zhang (2022), the estimated-double-score layer is likewise
  built from Assumptions~A2--A4 plus a martingale decomposition, a joint
  local Gaussian limit, Le Cam's third lemma, and a discretization
  device, rather than from a direct smooth plug-in Taylor expansion of
  the matching map.
\end{itemize}

The present appendix mirrors that same theorem architecture.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Assumptions~2.1--2.5 and the shared score-space regularity conditions
  in Section~4 are the primitive design / support / smoothness / moment
  layer.
\item
  Lemmas~4.1--4.12 together with the cell-specific lemmas in
  Sections~5--8 form the derived fixed-\(M\) matching layer: the
  two-dimensional score-radius rate, exact discrepancy order, exact
  matching-frequency rewrites, exact Hájek heterogeneity terms, and the
  residual martingale representation are all proved there rather than
  assumed in the theorem statements.
\item
  Proposition~4.8C plus the scenario-specific Propositions~5.4A,
  6.4A, 7.5A, and~8.5A provide one explicit primitive sufficient route
  from the theorem-level estimator objects to the closed variance
  formulas in the four cell-specific centered known-score CLTs.
\item
  Section~10 and Section~10.1B provide the extra estimated-score local
  experiment layer, again in the same non-smooth Le Cam style used in
  the benchmark feasible-score papers.
\end{enumerate}

Thus the current appendix now has \emph{two} theorem paths for the known-score layer.
First, the most general exact-share theorems keep the remaining exact-share closure objects visible as Closure Inputs~4.7A, 5.4, 6.4, 7.5, and~8.5. Second, Corollaries~5.6B, 6.6B, 7.7B, and~8.7B collect the benchmark-style \emph{primitive sufficient route} by combining Proposition~4.8C with the corresponding scenario-specific stabilization proposition. This is exactly the reason those new corollaries are included: they give a complete cell-by-cell theorem path whose hypotheses are stated at the same level of generality and proof granularity as the benchmark matching appendices, while the more general exact-share statements are still retained separately.

\section{1. Overview of the four-cell theorem structure and remaining closure conditions}\label{consensus-corrections-and-theorem-status-ledger}

This revision enforces the following corrections.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  The exact reuse algebra is derived from the exact within-match survey-weight-normalized
  imputation rule. The equal-share proxy reuse factor is removed from
  the main theory.
\item
  The notation shortcut that compressed treatment-specific
  inverse-inclusion weights into a single symbol is removed. Every
  formula displays the actual weight it uses: either \(w_Z(\mathbf X)\),
  \(w_z(\mathbf X)\), or \(w(\mathbf X)\).
\item
  The PATT score is written simply as \[\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_0(\mathbf X))\] with
  no extra superscript.
\item
  The prognostic score is denoted by \(\Psi\). The conditional mean
  functions are denoted by \(\mu\).
\item
  Every decomposition with a \(+B_n\) convention uses the corrected
  \textbf{focal-unit-minus-matched-unit} discrepancy sign.
\item
  The matching problem is treated as genuinely two-dimensional, so the
  score-radius part of the argument is based on dimension \(2\).
\item
  Matched-arm score indexing is no longer described as minimal or
  universally necessary. In any place where it appears, it is described
  only as a \textbf{sufficient} device for the proof route under
  discussion.
\item
  The estimated-score layer is now split explicitly into two
  levels. Section~10.1B records the ordinary survey-weighted estimating-equation
  linearization on the generic sandwich scale. Section~10 then moves to
  the score-normalized local-likelihood scale used by Abadie--Imbens and
  Yang--Zhang for the actual Le Cam argument. The manuscript therefore
  no longer mixes a generic weighted Jacobian with the likelihood-ratio
  shift.
\end{enumerate}

The resulting theorem structure is therefore:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Known-score retrospective PATE}: fully derived under explicit
  assumptions. Exact estimator, exact reuse algebra, exact matching-frequency
  rewrite, exact residual variance, exact centered CLT.
\item
  \textbf{Known-score retrospective PATT}: fully derived under explicit
  assumptions. One-sided control-score theorem with explicit one-arm
  stabilization conditions.
\item
  \textbf{Known-score prospective PATE}: fully derived under explicit
  assumptions. Common-weight specialization written out explicitly.
\item
  \textbf{Known-score prospective PATT}: fully derived under explicit
  assumptions. One-sided common-weight specialization written out
  explicitly.
\item
  \textbf{Estimated-score fixed-law theory}: conditional. Requires a
  weighted non-smooth perturbation theorem.
\item
  \textbf{Estimated-score changing-law theory}: conditional. The second
  adjustment block is recorded correctly, but still depends on the same
  perturbation theorem.
\item
  \textbf{Survey-weighted balancing / multiple robustness on reduced
  score space}: open. Not proved here.
\item
  \textbf{Primitive stabilization from lower-level geometric
  conditions}: open. Exact CLTs are stated under explicit stabilization
  conditions.
\item
  \textbf{Full design-based complex-survey asymptotics}: open. The
  present note is selected-sample i.i.d.
\end{itemize}
In the known-score layer, the explicit survey-weighted matching-frequency variance formulas use the shared exact-share moment-map convergence condition (Closure Input 4.7A), and the centered known-score CLTs additionally use the cell-specific coefficient-square stabilization conditions (Closure Inputs 5.4, 6.4, 7.5, 8.5).

\textbf{Final update on primitive assumptions versus closure conditions.} Relative to the immediately preceding working version, the present version removes theorem-level assumptions for the score-radius rate and for the heterogeneity central limit theorem in all four known-score cells. Those pieces are now proved as Lemma 4.3, Lemma 4.5, Lemmas 5.5/6.5/7.6/8.6, and the shared martingale-array Lemma 4.2. What remain explicit in the known-score layer are: (i) the shared exact-share moment-map convergence condition (Closure Input 4.7A), which is needed to close the exact survey-weighted matching-frequency first and second moment formulas without collapsing the exact within-match survey-weight-normalized share to an equal-share proxy, and (ii) the cell-specific exact coefficient-square stabilization conditions (Closure Inputs 5.4, 6.4, 7.5, 8.5), which are needed to close the residual-array quadratic-variation step on the exact coefficient scale. Proposition~4.8C in the main text now records a weaker local conditional matched-side survey-weight-law sufficient route for Closure Input~4.7A without imposing deterministic score sufficiency of the survey weight. Proposition~4.12C together with Propositions~5.4A, 6.4A, 7.5A, and~8.5A, proved in detail again in Appendix~P, records one optional equal-share validation subclass for the residual-variance closures by combining Corollary~4.8A with the published two-dimensional fixed-\(M\) coefficient-square geometry of Abadie--Imbens and Chen--Han. Likewise, in the estimated-score layer the note keeps only the Abadie--Imbens / Yang--Zhang style local-experiment assumptions, adds the required local-uniform clause for the local experiment, and does not smuggle in a smooth estimated-score expansion. The actual estimated-score estimator is recovered by a separate corollary rather than by silently identifying it with the discretized score device.

This detailed revision also adds Imported Lemma~4.2B, which records the standard stable refinement of the martingale CLT relative to the initial sigma-field, Proposition~4.8C, which gives a weaker local conditional matched-side survey-weight-law route for the exact-share moment maps, the scenario-specific Propositions~5.4A, 6.4A, 7.5A, and~8.5A, which move the optional equal-share validation route into the corresponding scenario sections, and Corollaries~5.6B, 6.6B, 7.7B, and~8.7B, which make the fully closed benchmark-style theorem path explicit cell by cell. the expanded in-place known-score proofs then rewrites the four known-score centered-CLT proofs with these closure devices displayed explicitly.



\subsection{1A. Benchmark grouping of the remaining nonprimitive inputs}\label{benchmark-grouping-of-the-remaining-nonprimitive-inputs}

For comparison with the lean benchmark skeleton, the detailed theorem stack below groups its remaining nonprimitive inputs as follows.

\begin{itemize}
\item
  \textbf{Closure Input C1 (exact-share closure on the matched-arm score space).} In the detailed notation this is Closure Input~4.7A. It closes the first- and second-moment limits of the exact matched unit-share maps on the matched-arm score space without replacing the exact within-match survey-weight-normalized share by an equal-share proxy. Proposition~4.8C records a weaker local conditional matched-side survey-weight-law sufficient route for this closure that still allows residual survey-weight heterogeneity inside one fixed score cell.
\item
  \textbf{Closure Input C2 (exact coefficient-square residual stabilization).} In the detailed cell-by-cell notation these are Closure Inputs~5.4, 6.4, 7.5, and~8.5. They close the predictable quadratic variation and the Lyapunov remainder for the exact residual array on the exact coefficient scale. Proposition~4.12C together with the scenario-specific Propositions~5.4A, 6.4A, 7.5A, and~8.5A, with full details in Appendix~P, gives one optional equal-share validation route for C2, so C2 is no longer left as a bare shortcut on that optional route.
\item
  \textbf{Regularity E1 (ordinary smooth survey-weighted first-step regularity).} This is the ordinary weighted M-estimation / LAN component recorded in Closure Input~10.1(1). Section~10.1B records a sufficient low-level survey-weighted M-estimation block implying this first-step piece one quantity at a time.
\item
  \textbf{Closure Input E2 (matching-specific non-smooth estimated-score perturbation).} This is the remaining frontier block in Closure Input~10.1(2)--(4), together with the imported Le Cam and Andreou--Werker probability devices stated in Section~10.1A.
\end{itemize}

This grouping is purely organizational. The detailed proofs still display the cell-specific formulas one quantity at a time, but the theorem-status boundary is the same as in the benchmark manuscripts: primitive framework assumptions first, proved known-score lemmas second, and only the remaining exact-share closure and non-smooth estimated-score closure kept explicit as separate inputs. In that sense, Assumptions~2.1--2.5 and~4.4 play the role of the primitive support/overlap/smoothness blocks in Abadie--Imbens (2006), while Closure Input~10.1 plays the role of the extra local-experiment layer used in Abadie--Imbens (2016) and Yang--Zhang (2022).

\subsection{1B. Fixed-law main theorems and the Yang--Zhang-style moving-target extension}\label{main-fixed-law-theorem-default-versus-the-yang-zhang-style-moving-target-extension}

The main scientific targets of the present manuscript are the fixed-law population PATE and PATT. Accordingly, Proposition~10.3 proves that under fixed law the target derivative block is zero in all four cells. This is the default theorem interpretation for the main estimated-score variance statements.

At the same time, Yang--Zhang-style estimated-score matching theory also studies a moving reduced-score target map under local perturbations. Proposition~10.5A and Theorem~10.6 record that distinct conditional extension explicitly. Under that moving-target route the second block
\[
\gamma_{A,2}^{\top}\Sigma_{A,\theta}\gamma_{A,2}
\]
need not vanish merely because the cell is labeled PATE or PATT; it vanishes only under fixed law or another local target-flatness restriction. The present manuscript therefore keeps both interpretations explicit instead of mixing them.

To make the theorem body read scenario by scenario, Sections~5--8 are now the primary theorem bundles. Each of those four sections collects, for a single scenario, the exact estimator, the known-score decomposition, the centered known-score limit, the estimated-score fixed-law limit, the score-indexed moving-target extension, and the corresponding linearization-based bootstrap formula. Section~10 then serves only as the shared non-smooth proof infrastructure behind those scenario-specific theorem statements.

\textbf{Remark 1.2A} (Where to read the fully closed theorem path first). Readers who want the theorem path written with the smallest possible number of nonprimitive labels should read each scenario section in the following order: first the exact estimator and decomposition, then Proposition~4.8C for the exact-share moment-map closure, then the scenario-specific stabilization proposition (Proposition~5.4A, 6.4A, 7.5A, or~8.5A), and finally Corollary~5.6B, 6.6B, 7.7B, or~8.7B. Those four corollaries were added precisely to collect the benchmark-style primitive sufficient route cell by cell. The closure-labeled theorems remain in place because they are the more general exact-share statements, but the new corollaries make explicit that the appendix no longer asks the reader to stop at a theorem-level closure condition whenever a proved sufficient route has already been supplied.

\section{2. Setup, designs, estimands, and score
notation}\label{setup-designs-estimands-and-score-notation}

\subsection{2.1 Superpopulation law and selected
sample}\label{superpopulation-law-and-selected-sample}

Let \[(Y(0),Y(1),X,Z,S)\] be defined on a superpopulation law
\(P_{\Omega}\). Here \(Z\in\{0,1\}\) is treatment and \(S\in\{0,1\}\) is
the survey-selection indicator. The analyst observes
\[(Y,X,Z)\quad\text{only when }S=1.\] The selected-sample law is
\[P_s(\cdot):=P_{\Omega}(\cdot\mid S=1).\] Throughout the note the
observed sample \[\{(Y_i,\mathbf X_i,Z_i)\}_{i=1}^n\] is i.i.d. from \(P_s\).

\textbf{Assumption 2.1} (Selected-sample i.i.d.). The observed sample
consists of i.i.d. draws from \(P_s\).

\subsection{2.2 Sampling designs}\label{sampling-designs}

\subsubsection{Retrospective sampling}\label{retrospective-sampling}

\textbf{Assumption 2.2} (Retrospective sampling).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  The inclusion probability may depend on \((X,Z)\):
  \[\pi_z(x):=P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid X=x,Z=z),\qquad z\in\{0,1\}.\]
\item
  The positivity condition holds: \[0<\pi_z(\mathbf X)<1\quad\text{a.s.}\]
\item
  Selection is conditionally independent of potential outcomes given
  \((X,Z)\): \[(Y(0),Y(1))\perp S\mid (X,Z).\]
\item
  Define the treatment-specific inverse-inclusion weights
  \[w_z(x):=\pi_z(x)^{-1},\qquad z\in\{0,1\}.\]
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{Prospective sampling}\label{prospective-sampling}

\textbf{Assumption 2.3} (Prospective sampling).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  The inclusion probability depends only on \(X\):
  \[\pi(x):=P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid X=x).\]
\item
  The positivity condition holds: \[0<\pi(\mathbf X)<1\quad\text{a.s.}\]
\item
  Selection is conditionally independent of treatment and potential
  outcomes given \(X\): \[(Z,Y(0),Y(1))\perp S\mid X.\]
\item
  Define the common inverse-inclusion weight \[w(x):=\pi(x)^{-1}.\]
\end{enumerate}

\subsection{2.3 Treatment assumptions and
estimands}\label{treatment-assumptions-and-estimands}

\subsubsection{PATE route}\label{pate-route}

\textbf{Assumption 2.4} (PATE treatment assumptions).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  Strong ignorability holds: \[(Y(0),Y(1))\perp Z\mid X.\]
\item
  Overlap holds: \[0<e(\mathbf X):=P_{\Omega}(Z=1\mid \mathbf X)<1\quad\text{a.s.}\]
\end{enumerate}

Define the conditional mean functions
\[\mu_z(\mathbf X):=E_{\Omega}[Y(z)\mid \mathbf X],\qquad z\in\{0,1\},\] and the
conditional effect \[\tau(\mathbf X):=\mu_1(\mathbf X)-\mu_0(\mathbf X).\] The population
average treatment effect is
\[\tau_{\mathrm{PATE}}:=E_{\Omega}[Y(1)-Y(0)]=E_{\Omega}[\tau(\mathbf X)].\]

\subsubsection{PATT route}\label{patt-route}

\textbf{Assumption 2.5} (PATT treatment assumptions).

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  One-sided treatment ignorability holds: \[Y(0)\perp Z\mid X.\]
\item
  One-sided overlap holds:
  \[P_{\Omega}(Z=1\mid \mathbf X)>0\Longrightarrow P_{\Omega}(Z=0\mid \mathbf X)>0.\]
\end{enumerate}

Define the treated-side conditional mean
\[\mu_{1\mid 1}(\mathbf X):=E_{\Omega}[Y(1)\mid \mathbf X,Z=1],\] the control
conditional mean \[\mu_0(\mathbf X):=E_{\Omega}[Y(0)\mid \mathbf X],\] and the
treated-specific conditional effect
\[\tau_T(\mathbf X):=\mu_{1\mid 1}(\mathbf X)-\mu_0(\mathbf X).\] The population average
treatment effect on the treated is
\[\tau_{\mathrm{PATT}}:=E_{\Omega}[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid Z=1].\] Under
Assumption 2.5, \[\tau_{\mathrm{PATT}}=E_{\Omega}[\tau_T(\mathbf X)\mid Z=1].\]

\subsection{2.4 Prognostic scores and known-score double
scores}\label{prognostic-scores-and-known-score-double-scores}

The prognostic score is denoted by \(\Psi\) and the regression mean is
denoted by \(\mu\). These two objects are kept notationally distinct
throughout.

For PATE, the arm-specific matching score is
\[\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X):=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_z(\mathbf X))\in\mathbb R^2,
\qquad z\in\{0,1\}.\] In the \textbf{known-score PATE specialization} used in
the main theorem path, \[\Psi_z(\mathbf X)=\mu_z(\mathbf X),
\qquad z\in\{0,1\},\] so that, only after that specialization is
invoked, \[\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\mu_z(\mathbf X)).\]

For PATT, the one-sided matching score is
\[\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X):=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_0(\mathbf X))\in\mathbb R^2.\] In the \textbf{known-score
one-sided PATT specialization} used in the main theorem path,
\[\Psi_0(\mathbf X)=\mu_0(\mathbf X),\] so that, only after that specialization is
invoked, \[\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\mu_0(\mathbf X)).\]

\textbf{Remark 2.5A} (Mean-score specialization is additional, not
automatic). The identities \(\Psi_z(\mathbf X)=\mu_z(\mathbf X)\) and
\(\Psi_0(\mathbf X)=\mu_0(\mathbf X)\) are \emph{not} implied by ignorability alone.
They are extra sufficient conditions ensuring that the conditional law
of the relevant potential outcome depends on \(X\) through the mean
score used in the theorem path. Whenever later displays specialize
\(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\) to \((e(\mathbf X),\mu_z(\mathbf X))\) or \(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)\) to \((e(\mathbf X),\mu_0(\mathbf X))\),
this additional prognostic-sufficiency condition is in force.

\subsection{2.5 Residual notation}\label{residual-notation}

For PATE define
\[\varepsilon_{z,i}:=Y_i-\mu_z(\mathbf X_i)\qquad\text{when }Z_i=z,\] and
\[\sigma_z^2(\mathbf X_i):=\operatorname{Var}_{\Omega}(Y(z)\mid \mathbf X_i).\]

For PATT define the treated residual under the one-sided route by
\[\varepsilon_{1\mid 1,i}:=Y_i-\mu_{1\mid 1}(\mathbf X_i)\qquad\text{when }Z_i=1,\]
and
\[\sigma_{1\mid 1}^2(\mathbf X_i):=\operatorname{Var}_{\Omega}(Y(1)\mid \mathbf X_i,Z_i=1).\]
The control residual remains
\[\varepsilon_{0,i}:=Y_i-\mu_0(\mathbf X_i)\qquad\text{when }Z_i=0,\] with
variance \[\sigma_0^2(\mathbf X_i):=\operatorname{Var}_{\Omega}(Y(0)\mid \mathbf X_i).\]

\section{3. Population-recovery identities and score
relations}\label{population-recovery-identities-and-score-relations}

\subsection{3.1 Retrospective recovery
identities}\label{retrospective-recovery-identities}

\textbf{Lemma 3.1} (Retrospective recovery identity). Under Assumption
2.2, for every integrable random variable \[H=H(X,Z,Y(0),Y(1))\] we have
\[E_s\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)H\right]
=
\frac{E_{\Omega}[H]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}.\] Consequently,
\[\tau_{\mathrm{PATE}}
=
\frac{E_s\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\right]}{E_s\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)\right]},\]
and \[\tau_{\mathrm{PATT}}
=
\frac{E_s\!\left[Z\,w_1(\mathbf X)\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\right]}{E_s\!\left[Z\,w_1(\mathbf X)\right]}.\]

\textbf{Proof.} We change one object at a time: \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)H\right]
&=
E_{\Omega}\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)H\mid S=1\right]\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[S\,w_Z(\mathbf X)H\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[E_{\Omega}\!\left\{S\,w_Z(\mathbf X)H\mid \mathbf X,Z,Y(0),Y(1)\right\}\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)H\,E_{\Omega}\!\left\{S\mid \mathbf X,Z,Y(0),Y(1)\right\}\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)H\,E_{\Omega}(S\mid \mathbf X,Z)\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)H\,\pi_Z(\mathbf X)\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}[H]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}.
\end{aligned}\] Now choose \(H=Y(1)-Y(0)\) and divide by the same
identity with \(H\equiv 1\): \[\begin{aligned}
\frac{E_s\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\right]}{E_s\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)\right]}
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}[Y(1)-Y(0)]/P_{\Omega}(S=1)}{1/P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
E_{\Omega}[Y(1)-Y(0)]\\
&=
\tau_{\mathrm{PATE}}.
\end{aligned}\] Next choose \(H=Z\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\) and divide by the same
identity with \(H=Z\): \[\begin{aligned}
\frac{E_s\!\left[Z\,w_1(\mathbf X)\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\right]}{E_s\!\left[Z\,w_1(\mathbf X)\right]}
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}[Z\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}]/P_{\Omega}(S=1)}{E_{\Omega}[Z]/P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}[Z\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}]}{P_{\Omega}(Z=1)}\\
&=
E_{\Omega}[Y(1)-Y(0)\mid Z=1]\\
&=
\tau_{\mathrm{PATT}}.
\end{aligned}\] \(\square\)

\subsection{3.2 Prospective recovery
identities}\label{prospective-recovery-identities}

\textbf{Lemma 3.2} (Prospective recovery identity). Under Assumption
2.3, for every integrable random variable \[H=H(X,Z,Y(0),Y(1))\] we have
\[E_s\!\left[w(\mathbf X)H\right]
=
\frac{E_{\Omega}[H]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}.\] Consequently,
\[\tau_{\mathrm{PATE}}
=
\frac{E_s\!\left[w(\mathbf X)\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\right]}{E_s\!\left[w(\mathbf X)\right]},\]
and \[\tau_{\mathrm{PATT}}
=
\frac{E_s\!\left[Z\,w(\mathbf X)\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\right]}{E_s\!\left[Z\,w(\mathbf X)\right]}.\]

\textbf{Proof.} We again change one object at a time: \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\!\left[w(\mathbf X)H\right]
&=
E_{\Omega}\!\left[w(\mathbf X)H\mid S=1\right]\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[S\,w(\mathbf X)H\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[E_{\Omega}\!\left\{S\,w(\mathbf X)H\mid \mathbf X,Z,Y(0),Y(1)\right\}\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[w(\mathbf X)H\,E_{\Omega}\!\left\{S\mid \mathbf X,Z,Y(0),Y(1)\right\}\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[w(\mathbf X)H\,E_{\Omega}(S\mid \mathbf X)\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}\!\left[w(\mathbf X)H\,\pi(\mathbf X)\right]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}\\
&=
\frac{E_{\Omega}[H]}{P_{\Omega}(S=1)}.
\end{aligned}\] Choosing \(H=Y(1)-Y(0)\) and \(H=Z\{Y(1)-Y(0)\}\) gives
the two displayed ratio formulas by the same steps as in Lemma 3.1.
\(\square\)

\subsection{3.3 Selected-sample propensity
relations}\label{selected-sample-propensity-relations}

\textbf{Lemma 3.3} (Retrospective Bayes relation for the propensity
score). Under Assumption 2.2, \[\frac{e(\mathbf X)}{1-e(\mathbf X)}
=
\frac{w_1(\mathbf X)}{w_0(\mathbf X)}\,
\frac{e_s(\mathbf X)}{1-e_s(\mathbf X)},\] where \[e_s(\mathbf X):=P_s(Z=1\mid \mathbf X).\]
Equivalently, \[e(\mathbf X)
=
\frac{w_1(\mathbf X)e_s(\mathbf X)}{w_1(\mathbf X)e_s(\mathbf X)+w_0(\mathbf X)\{1-e_s(\mathbf X)\}}.\]

\textbf{Proof.} Start from Bayes' rule and change one object at a time:
\[\begin{aligned}
e_s(\mathbf X)
&=
P_{\Omega}(Z=1\mid \mathbf X,S=1)\\
&=
\frac{P_{\Omega}(Z=1,S=1\mid \mathbf X)}{P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid \mathbf X)}\\
&=
\frac{P_{\Omega}(Z=1\mid \mathbf X)P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid \mathbf X,Z=1)}{P_{\Omega}(Z=1\mid \mathbf X)P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid \mathbf X,Z=1)+P_{\Omega}(Z=0\mid \mathbf X)P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid \mathbf X,Z=0)}\\
&=
\frac{e(\mathbf X)\pi_1(\mathbf X)}{e(\mathbf X)\pi_1(\mathbf X)+\{1-e(\mathbf X)\}\pi_0(\mathbf X)}.
\end{aligned}\] Multiply both sides by the denominator:
\[\begin{aligned}
e_s(\mathbf X)\bigl[e(\mathbf X)\pi_1(\mathbf X)+\{1-e(\mathbf X)\}\pi_0(\mathbf X)\bigr]
&=
e(\mathbf X)\pi_1(\mathbf X).
\end{aligned}\] Move the first term on the left involving
\(e(\mathbf X)\pi_1(\mathbf X)\) to the right: \[\begin{aligned}
e_s(\mathbf X)\{1-e(\mathbf X)\}\pi_0(\mathbf X)
&=
e(\mathbf X)\pi_1(\mathbf X)\{1-e_s(\mathbf X)\}.
\end{aligned}\] Divide both sides by \(\{1-e(\mathbf X)\}\pi_1(\mathbf X)\{1-e_s(\mathbf X)\}\):
\[\begin{aligned}
\frac{e(\mathbf X)}{1-e(\mathbf X)}
&=
\frac{\pi_0(\mathbf X)^{-1}}{\pi_1(\mathbf X)^{-1}}\,\frac{e_s(\mathbf X)}{1-e_s(\mathbf X)}\\
&=
\frac{w_1(\mathbf X)}{w_0(\mathbf X)}\,\frac{e_s(\mathbf X)}{1-e_s(\mathbf X)}.
\end{aligned}\] This gives the odds form. Solving the odds identity for
\(e(\mathbf X)\) gives the displayed formula. \(\square\)

\textbf{Lemma 3.4} (Prospective propensity identity). Under Assumption
2.3, \[e_s(\mathbf X)=e(\mathbf X).\]

\textbf{Proof.} Change one object at a time: \[\begin{aligned}
e_s(\mathbf X)
&=
P_{\Omega}(Z=1\mid \mathbf X,S=1)\\
&=
\frac{P_{\Omega}(Z=1,S=1\mid \mathbf X)}{P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid \mathbf X)}\\
&=
\frac{P_{\Omega}(Z=1\mid \mathbf X)P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid \mathbf X,Z=1)}{P_{\Omega}(S=1\mid \mathbf X)}\\
&=
\frac{e(\mathbf X)\pi(\mathbf X)}{\pi(\mathbf X)}\\
&=
e(\mathbf X).
\end{aligned}\] \(\square\)

\subsection{3.4 Honest score-estimation rules under the four
cells}\label{score-estimation-rules-under-the-four-cells}

The current consensus across the benchmark derivations is the following.

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  In the retrospective cells the first-step propensity problem is
  \emph{not} the same as the prospective one. One admissible route is to
  estimate the selected-sample propensity \(e_s(\mathbf X)\) from the selected
  sample and then translate it to the population propensity \(e(\mathbf X)\) by
  the Bayes relation in Lemma 3.3. An equivalent route is to
  parameterize \(e(\mathbf X)\) directly and use a treatment-specific weighted
  pseudo-score or pseudo-likelihood. In either route, the
  treatment-specific survey weights enter the retrospective propensity
  step.
\item
  In the prospective cells \(e_s(\mathbf X)=e(\mathbf X)\), so ordinary selected-sample
  propensity fitting is appropriate.
\item
  For the prognostic score, the known-score object is the generic reduced
  score \(\Psi_z(\mathbf X)\) in PATE and the generic one-sided reduced score
  \(\Psi_0(\mathbf X)\) in PATT. The mean-score identities
  \(\Psi_z(\mathbf X)=\mu_z(\mathbf X)\) and \(\Psi_0(\mathbf X)=\mu_0(\mathbf X)\) are additional
  sufficient specializations used only when the known-score theorem path is
  written on the mean-score scale.
\item
  Under the selected-sample i.i.d. superpopulation used here, within-arm
  outcome regressions may be estimated from the selected sample without
  additional survey weighting for consistency, because selection is
  conditionally independent of the relevant potential outcome once
  \((X,Z)\) is fixed. Survey-weighted outcome regressions are also
  admissible, but then their effect belongs to the nuisance-score law,
  the estimating function \(U_A\), and the LAN block rather than to the
  known-score identification step itself.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Remark 3.5} (What reduced-score balancing is used and what is
not used here). The present survey-weighted known-score theory uses two
different facts.

First, it uses the ordinary \(X\)-level causal conditions: treatment
ignorability for PATE and one-sided control ignorability for PATT.
Second, it uses the selected-sample Hájek recovery identities generated
by the explicit inverse-inclusion weights. It does \textbf{not} require
any additional sampling lemma of the form
\[S\perp \{Y(0),Y(1)\}\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)
\qquad\text{or}\qquad
S\perp Y(0)\mid \mathbf D_0(\mathbf X).\] Those statements are stronger than what is
needed for the four-cell known-score theory proved here.

For completeness, the ordinary treatment-balancing consequence of the
known-score double score may still be imported unchanged from the unweighted
DSM literature.

\textbf{Imported Lemma 3.6} (Known-score double-score treatment balancing;
unchanged from Yang--Zhang). Under treatment ignorability
\((Y(0),Y(1))\perp Z\mid X\), if the known-score score for arm \(z\) is
\[\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_z(\mathbf X))\] with \(e(\mathbf X)=P_\Omega(Z=1\mid \mathbf X)\), then
\[Y(z)\perp Z\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X).\] Under one-sided PATT identification
\(Y(0)\perp Z\mid X\), \[Y(0)\perp Z\mid \mathbf D_0(\mathbf X).\]

\textbf{Proof.} We prove the PATE statement; the one-sided PATT
statement is the same argument with \(z=0\). Because \(e(\mathbf X)\) is one
coordinate of \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\), \[E_\Omega[Z\mid \mathbf X,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)]
=
E_\Omega[Z\mid \mathbf X]
=
e(\mathbf X).\] Also, \[E_\Omega[Z\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)]
=
E_\Omega\!\bigl[E_\Omega(Z\mid \mathbf X,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X))\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\bigr]
=
E_\Omega\!\bigl[e(\mathbf X)\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\bigr]
=
e(\mathbf X),\] because \(e(\mathbf X)\) is measurable with respect to \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\).
Therefore \[P_\Omega(Z=1\mid \mathbf X,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X))
=
P_\Omega(Z=1\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)),\] which means \(X\perp Z\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\). Now let
\(A\) be any Borel set. Then \[\begin{aligned}
P_\Omega\{Y(z)\in A\mid Z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\}
&=
E_\Omega\bigl[P_\Omega\{Y(z)\in A\mid \mathbf X,Z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\}\mid Z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\bigr]\\
&=
E_\Omega\bigl[P_\Omega\{Y(z)\in A\mid \mathbf X,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\}\mid Z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\bigr]\\
&=
E_\Omega\bigl[P_\Omega\{Y(z)\in A\mid X\}\mid Z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\bigr]\\
&=
E_\Omega\bigl[P_\Omega\{Y(z)\in A\mid X\}\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\bigr]\\
&=
P_\Omega\{Y(z)\in A\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\}.
\end{aligned}\] Thus \(Y(z)\perp Z\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\). \(\square\)

\textbf{Lemma 3.6A} (Score-space rewrite under the mean-score specialization).
Suppose the known-score mean-score specialization is in force.

For a PATE cell, for each arm \(z\in\{0,1\}\), define the selected-sample
score-space regression
\[m_{s,z}(d):=E_s[Y\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=d,Z=z].\]
Then, on the event \(\{Z=z\}\),
\[m_{s,z}(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X))=\mu_z(\mathbf X)\qquad\text{a.s.}
\]
Consequently,
\[\tau(\mathbf X)=m_{s,1}(\mathbf D_1(\mathbf X))-m_{s,0}(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X))\qquad\text{a.s.}
\]
and for every exact within-match survey-weight-normalized coefficient system
\(\{\lambda_{i\to j}^{(1-z),\mathrm{ex}}\}\),
\[\mu_{1-z}(\mathbf X_i)-\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(1-z),\mathrm{ex}}\mu_{1-z}(\mathbf X_j)
=
m_{s,1-z}(D_{1-z}(\mathbf X_i))-
\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(1-z),\mathrm{ex}}m_{s,1-z}(D_{1-z}(\mathbf X_j)).\]

For the one-sided PATT cell, define
\[m_{s,0}(d):=E_s[Y\mid \mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)=d,Z=0].\]
Then, on \(\{Z=0\}\),
\[m_{s,0}(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X))=\mu_0(\mathbf X)\qquad\text{a.s.},\]
so that
\[\tau_T(\mathbf X)=\mu_{1\mid 1}(\mathbf X)-m_{s,0}(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X))\qquad\text{a.s.}
\]
and, for every exact one-sided coefficient system
\(\{\lambda_{i\to j}^{(0),\mathrm{ex}}\}\),
\[\mu_0(\mathbf X_i)-\sum_{j=1}^n\lambda_{i\to j}^{(0),\mathrm{ex}}\mu_0(\mathbf X_j)
=
m_{s,0}(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X_i))-
\sum_{j=1}^n\lambda_{i\to j}^{(0),\mathrm{ex}}m_{s,0}(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X_j)).\]
Therefore every known-score heterogeneity block, every known-score bias block, and
all corresponding probability-limit formulas in Sections~5--8 may be
written either on the \(X\)-regression scale or, equivalently, on the
relevant double-score scale.

\textbf{Proof.} We prove the PATE identity for one arm \(z\); the other arm
and the one-sided PATT statement are the same calculation with the
relevant score substituted.

Because \(Y=Y(z)\) on \(\{Z=z\}\),
\[\begin{aligned}
m_{s,z}(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X))
&=
E_s[Y\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X),Z=z]\\
&=
E_s[Y(z)\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X),Z=z]\\
&=
E_s\!\bigl[E_s\{Y(z)\mid \mathbf X,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X),Z=z\}\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X),Z=z\bigr]\\
&=
E_s\!\bigl[E_s\{Y(z)\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X),Z=z\bigr]\\
&=
E_s\!\bigl[E_\Omega\{Y(z)\mid X\}\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X),Z=z\bigr]\\
&=
E_s\!\bigl[\mu_z(\mathbf X)\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X),Z=z\bigr]\\
&=
\mu_z(\mathbf X).
\end{aligned}\]
The last equality holds because under the mean-score specialization,
\(\mu_z(\mathbf X)\) is one coordinate of \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\mu_z(\mathbf X))\), hence is
measurable with respect to \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\). Subtract the two arm-specific
identities to get
\[\tau(\mathbf X)=m_{s,1}(\mathbf D_1(\mathbf X))-m_{s,0}(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)).\]
Now substitute only one object at a time into the matching discrepancy:
\[\begin{aligned}
&\mu_{1-z}(\mathbf X_i)-\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(1-z),\mathrm{ex}}\mu_{1-z}(\mathbf X_j)\\
&=
m_{s,1-z}(D_{1-z}(\mathbf X_i))-
\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(1-z),\mathrm{ex}}m_{s,1-z}(D_{1-z}(\mathbf X_j)).
\end{aligned}\]
The one-sided PATT identity is identical with \(z=0\) and
\(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\mu_0(\mathbf X))\). \(\square\)

A stronger survey-weighted reduced-score balancing theorem on the score
space would be useful for multiple-robust identification, but it remains
outside the proved core of the present note and is therefore not assumed
here.



\subsection{3.4A What the inner survey-weighted matched-set mean targets}\label{what-the-inner-survey-weighted-matched unit-mean-targets}

The next lemma records explicitly the object targeted by the inner
survey-weighted matched unit average. This is the point at which matching on the
correct population score and survey-weighted within-match normalization play
different roles: the score fixes the \emph{conditioning variable}, while
the survey weight of the matched units fix the \emph{probability law inside the score
cell}.

\textbf{Lemma 3.6B} (Survey-weighted matched unit mean recovers the population
score-space mean). Fix a treatment arm \(z\in\{0,1\}\).

\emph{Retrospective cells.} Define
\[
m_{\Omega,z}^{R}(d)
:=
\frac{E_s[w_z(\mathbf X)Y\mid Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=d]}{E_s[w_z(\mathbf X)\mid Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=d]}.
\]
Under Assumptions 2.1 and 2.2, if the relevant treatment-balancing
statement \(Y(z)\perp Z\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\) holds (and in the one-sided PATT
cell only \(z=0\) is used), then
\[
m_{\Omega,z}^{R}(d)=E_\Omega[Y(z)\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=d].
\]

\emph{Prospective cells.} Define
\[
m_{\Omega,z}^{P}(d)
:=
\frac{E_s[w(\mathbf X)Y\mid Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=d]}{E_s[w(\mathbf X)\mid Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=d]}.
\]
Under Assumptions 2.1 and 2.3, if the relevant treatment-balancing
statement \(Y(z)\perp Z\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\) holds (and in the one-sided PATT
cell only \(z=0\) is used), then
\[
m_{\Omega,z}^{P}(d)=E_\Omega[Y(z)\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=d].
\]

\textbf{Proof.} We prove the retrospective statement. The prospective
statement is the same chain of equalities with \(\pi(\mathbf X)\) and \(w(\mathbf X)\)
replacing \(\pi_z(\mathbf X)\) and \(w_z(\mathbf X)\).

Write \(D:=\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\). Start from the selected-sample ratio:
\[
\begin{aligned}
\frac{E_s[w_z(\mathbf X)Y\mid Z=z,D]}{E_s[w_z(\mathbf X)\mid Z=z,D]}
&=
\frac{E[S\,w_z(\mathbf X)Y\mid Z=z,D]}{E[S\,w_z(\mathbf X)\mid Z=z,D]}\\
&=
\frac{E[S\,w_z(\mathbf X)Y(z)\mid Z=z,D]}{E[S\,w_z(\mathbf X)\mid Z=z,D]}\\
&=
\frac{E\!\left[E\{S\,w_z(\mathbf X)Y(z)\mid \mathbf X,Z=z,D\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}
     {E\!\left[E\{S\,w_z(\mathbf X)\mid \mathbf X,Z=z,D\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}\\
&=
\frac{E\!\left[E\{S\,w_z(\mathbf X)Y(z)\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}
     {E\!\left[E\{S\,w_z(\mathbf X)\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}\\
&=
\frac{E\!\left[w_z(\mathbf X)\,E\{S\,Y(z)\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}
     {E\!\left[w_z(\mathbf X)\,E\{S\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}\\
&=
\frac{E\!\left[w_z(\mathbf X)\,E\{S\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\,E\{Y(z)\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}
     {E\!\left[w_z(\mathbf X)\,E\{S\mid \mathbf X,Z=z\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}\\
&=
\frac{E\!\left[w_z(\mathbf X)\pi_z(\mathbf X)\,E_\Omega\{Y(z)\mid X\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}
     {E\!\left[w_z(\mathbf X)\pi_z(\mathbf X)\mid Z=z,D\right]}\\
&=
\frac{E\!\left[E_\Omega\{Y(z)\mid X\}\mid Z=z,D\right]}
     {E[1\mid Z=z,D]}\\
&=
E_\Omega[Y(z)\mid Z=z,D]\\
&=
E_\Omega[Y(z)\mid D].
\end{aligned}
\]
The first equality rewrites selected-sample conditional expectations by
the selection indicator \(S\). The second equality uses \(Y=Y(z)\) on
\(\{Z=z\}\). The fourth equality uses that \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\) is a measurable
function of \(X\). The seventh equality uses the retrospective sampling
assumption \(Y(z)\perp S\mid \mathbf X,Z\), together with treatment ignorability
for PATE or its one-sided \(Y(0)\perp Z\mid X\) version for PATT. The
eighth equality substitutes \(E(S\mid \mathbf X,Z=z)=\pi_z(\mathbf X)\) and
\(w_z(\mathbf X)=\pi_z(\mathbf X)^{-1}\). The last equality uses the relevant
treatment-balancing property from Imported Lemma 3.6. This proves the
displayed identity. \(\square\)

\textbf{Remark 3.6C} (What raw matched unit averaging targets, and why outer
focal unit weighting alone does not fix it). The raw selected-sample
matched unit mean satisfies
\[
E_s[Y\mid Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)]
=
E_s[Y(z)\mid S=1,Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)].
\]
Under the current known-score assumptions this is \emph{not} generally equal
to \(E_\Omega[Y(z)\mid \mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)]\). If one were to aggregate the raw matched unit
mean with the outer focal unit Hájek weight, the result would be
\[
\frac{E_s\!\left[w_Z(\mathbf X)\,E_s\{Y(z)\mid S=1,Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\}\right]}{E_s[w_Z(\mathbf X)]}
=
E_\Omega\!\left[E_s\{Y(z)\mid S=1,Z=z,\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\}\right],
\]
which equals \(E_\Omega[Y(z)]\) only if the inner selected-sample
score-cell mean already equals the population score-cell mean. Thus the
outer survey weighting and the inner survey weight of the matched uniting solve two
different problems.

In the special known-score mean-score case
\[
\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\mu_z(\mathbf X)),
\]
the second coordinate of \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\) is \(\mu_z(\mathbf X)\) itself, so both the
survey-weighted matched unit mean and the raw matched unit mean collapse to
\(\mu_z(\mathbf X)\) almost surely. This is why the raw known-score mean-score path
can still be consistent, even though the survey-weighted matched unit mean is
the correct minimal-assumption score-space target in the general
retrospective/prospective WDSM proof route.


\section{4. Shared exact-matching algebraic
tools}\label{shared-exact-matching-algebraic-tools}

\subsection{4.1 Exact within-match survey-weight-normalized coefficients}\label{exact-within-match survey-weight-normalized-within-match-coefficients}

The next definition is the exact building block that replaces every
equal-share proxy reuse object.

\textbf{Definition 4.1} (Exact within-match survey-weight-normalized within-match
coefficient). Let \(\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)\) denote the set of the \(M\)
nearest matched units in arm \(z\) for focal unit \(i\), measured on the
relevant score space. The exact within-match survey-weight-normalized within-match
coefficient is always defined as \[\lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}
:=
\frac{\text{survey weight of matched unit }j}{\text{sum of survey weights over the matched set over }\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)}\,
\mathbf 1\{j\in\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)\}.\] The survey weight of the matched unit inside the
fraction depends on the scenario:

\begin{itemize}
\item
  retrospective PATE: matched-unit survey weight \(w_z(\mathbf X_j)\);
\item
  prospective PATE: matched-unit survey weight \(w(\mathbf X_j)\);
\item
  retrospective PATT: matched-unit survey weight \(w_0(\mathbf X_j)\);
\item
  prospective PATT: matched-unit survey weight \(w(\mathbf X_j)\).
\end{itemize}

For each focal unit \(i\) the within-match survey-weight-normalized coefficients sum to one:
\[\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}=1.\]

\textbf{Proof.} Fix a focal unit \(i\). Then \[\begin{aligned}
\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}
&=
\sum_{j=1}^n
\frac{\text{survey weight of matched unit }j}{\sum_{k\in\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)} \text{survey weight of matched unit }k}
\mathbf 1\{j\in\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)\}\\
&=
\frac{\sum_{j\in\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)}\text{survey weight of matched unit }j}{\sum_{k\in\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)} \text{survey weight of matched unit }k}\\
&=1.
\end{aligned}\] \(\square\)

\subsection{4.2 A shared martingale-array CLT lemma for survey-weighted matching-frequency
residual
sums}\label{a-shared-martingale-array-clt-lemma-for-survey-weighted matching-frequency-residual-sums}

The next lemma is the probability tool used in all four known-score cells. It
is stated in martingale-array form because every matching-frequency residual
sum can be read in a deterministic sample order and therefore
represented as a square-integrable martingale difference array.

\textbf{Lemma 4.2} (Conditional martingale-array CLT with exact
survey-weighted matching-frequency coefficients). Let \(\mathcal G_n\) be the sigma-field
generated by all observed covariates, treatments, matching sets, and
exact within-match survey-weight-normalized coefficients. For each \(n\), let
\(\xi_{n,1},\dots,\xi_{n,m_n}\) be random variables and define the
filtration \[\mathcal F_{n,0}:=\mathcal G_n,
\qquad
\mathcal F_{n,\ell}:=\mathcal G_n\vee \sigma(\xi_{n,1},\dots,\xi_{n,\ell}),
\qquad 1\le \ell\le m_n.\] Assume that for every \(\ell\),
\[E(\xi_{n,\ell}\mid \mathcal F_{n,\ell-1})=0.\] Define the predictable
quadratic variation by
\[V_n:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}E(\xi_{n,\ell}^2\mid \mathcal F_{n,\ell-1}).\]
Assume that there exists \(\delta>0\) and a constant \(V\in(0,\infty)\)
such that \[V_n\to_p V,\] and
\[\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}E\bigl(|\xi_{n,\ell}|^{2+\delta}\mid \mathcal F_{n,\ell-1}\bigr)\to_p 0.\]
Then \[\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}\xi_{n,\ell}\Rightarrow N(0,V).\]

\textbf{Proof.} For each \(n\), the partial sums
\[M_{n,m}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m}\xi_{n,\ell},
\qquad 0\le m\le m_n,\] form a square-integrable martingale with respect
to \(\{\mathcal F_{n,m}\}_{m=0}^{m_n}\), because
\[E(M_{n,m}\mid \mathcal F_{n,m-1})
=
M_{n,m-1}+E(\xi_{n,m}\mid \mathcal F_{n,m-1})
=
M_{n,m-1}.\] Its predictable quadratic variation is
\[\langle M_n\rangle_{m_n}
=
\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}E(\xi_{n,\ell}^2\mid \mathcal F_{n,\ell-1})
=
V_n.\] By assumption, \(V_n\to_p V\). The Lyapunov condition implies the
conditional Lindeberg condition because for every \(\varepsilon>0\),
\[\begin{aligned}
&\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}E\bigl(\xi_{n,\ell}^2\mathbf 1\{|\xi_{n,\ell}|>\varepsilon\}\mid \mathcal F_{n,\ell-1}\bigr)\\
&\le
\varepsilon^{-\delta}
\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}E\bigl(|\xi_{n,\ell}|^{2+\delta}\mid \mathcal F_{n,\ell-1}\bigr)
\to_p 0.
\end{aligned}\] Therefore the martingale central limit theorem yields
\[M_{n,m_n}\Rightarrow N(0,V).\] This is exactly the displayed
conclusion. \(\square\)

\textbf{Remark 4.2A} (How the lemma is used in the four cells). In each
known-score cell, the matching-frequency residual term is written as
\[\sqrt n\,E_{n,A}^{\mathrm{ex}}
=
\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}\xi_{n,\ell}^{A},\] where the summands are ordered by
any deterministic indexing of the sampled units and \(\xi_{n,\ell}^{A}\)
is the coefficient multiplying the corresponding residual divided by the
relevant \(\sqrt n\)-scaled Hájek denominator. Because the coefficients
are \(\mathcal G_n\)-measurable and the residuals are conditionally mean
zero and independent across units, \(\{\xi_{n,\ell}^{A}\}\) is
automatically a martingale difference array with respect to the
filtration above. In the cell-specific CLT proofs below, we therefore
identify the predictable quadratic variation explicitly and then invoke
the lemma.



\textbf{Imported Lemma 4.2B} (Stable martingale CLT relative to the
initial sigma-field). In the setup of Lemma~4.2, suppose the limit
\(V\) is deterministic. Then the martingale sum
\[
M_n:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{m_n}\xi_{n,\ell}
\]
converges stably with respect to \(\mathcal F_{n,0}\) to a centered
Gaussian variable \(G\sim N(0,V)\). Equivalently, for every bounded
\(\mathcal F_{n,0}\)-measurable random variable \(H_n\) and every
\(t\in\mathbb R\),
\[
E\!\left[H_n e^{itM_n}\right]-E[H_n]e^{-t^2V/2}\to 0.
\]
Hence if \(W_n\) is \(\mathcal F_{n,0}\)-measurable and
\(W_n\Rightarrow W\), then
\[
(W_n,M_n)\Rightarrow (W,G),
\]
with \(G\) independent of \(W\).

\textbf{Status.} This is an imported standard strengthening of the
martingale CLT. The present note uses it only to combine the
\(\mathcal F_{n,0}\)-measurable heterogeneity / Hájek block with the
conditionally centered survey-weighted matching-frequency residual block. No survey-specific
modification is needed.

\textbf{Remark 4.2B.} In all four known-score cells, the initial sigma-field
\(\mathcal F_{n,0}\) may be taken to contain the sampled covariates,
treatment assignments, survey weights, and the matching graph. The
heterogeneity term is \(\mathcal F_{n,0}\)-measurable, while the matched unit
residual block is a martingale array relative to the same initial field.
Imported Lemma~4.2B is therefore exactly the closure device that turns
the separate heterogeneity CLT and residual CLT into the final centered
known-score Gaussian limit.


\subsection{4.3 A shared bias-order lemma from exact within-match survey-weight-normalized
coefficients and two-dimensional score
radii}\label{a-shared-bias-order-lemma-from-exact-within-match survey-weight-normalized-coefficients-and-two-dimensional-score-radii}

The exact bias argument does not need an equal-share proxy for the within-match coefficients. It
needs only two facts: exact within-match survey-weight-normalized coefficients sum to one,
and every matched matched unit lies in a shrinking two-dimensional score ball.

\textbf{Lemma 4.3} (Two-dimensional score-radius rate from fixed-\(M\) matching geometry). Fix any known-score matching problem used in the four cells and let \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)\) denote the corresponding matching score, of dimension two. Assume that on the relevant selected-sample arm the score support is compact and convex and the corresponding score density is continuous and bounded away from zero and infinity. Let
\[\rho_{n,z}(i):=\max_{j\in\mathcal J_{\mathbf D_z}(i)}\|D_z(\mathbf X_i)-D_z(\mathbf X_j)\|.\]
Then there is a finite constant \(C\), depending only on \(M\) and the score-space regularity constants, such that
\[\sup_i E_s\!\left[n\rho_{n,z}(i)^2\mid D_z(\mathbf X_i)\right]\le C.\]
Consequently, for every nonnegative focal unit weight sequence \(a_i\) satisfying
\[\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_i^2=O_p(1),\]
we have
\[\sqrt n\,\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_i\rho_{n,z}(i)=O_p(1).\]

\textbf{Proof.} On the two-dimensional score space, the fixed-\(M\) nearest-neighbor discrepancy lemmas of Abadie--Imbens apply with \(k=2\). Therefore the \(M\)-th matching discrepancy satisfies
\[E_s\!\left[\rho_{n,z}(i)^2\mid D_z(\mathbf X_i)\right]\le \frac{C}{n}\]
uniformly in \(i\). Multiply both sides by \(n\) to obtain the first display. For the averaged radius, change one object at a time:
\[\begin{aligned}
\sqrt n\,\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_i\rho_{n,z}(i)
&\le
\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n a_i^2\right)^{1/2}
\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n n\rho_{n,z}(i)^2\right)^{1/2}\\
&= O_p(1)\times
\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n n\rho_{n,z}(i)^2\right)^{1/2}.
\end{aligned}\]
It remains to control the second factor. By Markov's inequality,
\[\begin{aligned}
P\!\left(\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n n\rho_{n,z}(i)^2>t\right)
&\le
\frac1t E\!\left[\frac1n\sum_{i=1}^n n\rho_{n,z}(i)^2\right]\\
&=
\frac1{tn}\sum_{i=1}^n E\!\left[n\rho_{n,z}(i)^2\right]\\
&\le \frac{C}{t}.
\end{aligned}\]
Hence \(n^{-1}\sum_i n\rho_{n,z}(i)^2=O_p(1)\), and the displayed averaged-radius rate follows. \(\square\)

\textbf{Assumption 4.4} (Lipschitz outcome regressions in score space). In every matching problem used below, the relevant conditional mean function is Lipschitz in the matching score:
\[|\mu_z(x)-\mu_z(x')|\le L_z\|D_z(x)-D_z(x')\|\]
for PATE, and
\[|\mu_0(x)-\mu_0(x')|\le L_0\|\mathbf D_0(x)-\mathbf D_0(x')\|\]
for PATT.

\textbf{Lemma 4.5} (Exact discrepancy bound and root-\(n\) bias order). Fix a scenario and a focal unit \(i\). Let \(z\) denote the matched arm used to impute the missing potential outcome for that focal unit. Then
\[\left|\mu_z(\mathbf X_i)-\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\mu_z(\mathbf X_j)\right|
\le L_z\rho_{n,z}(i).\]
Consequently, in every cell the exact bias term is \(O_p(n^{-1/2})\) under Lemma 4.3 and Assumption 4.4.

\textbf{Proof.} We change one object at a time: \[\begin{aligned}
\left|\mu_z(\mathbf X_i)-\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\mu_z(\mathbf X_j)\right|
&=
\left|\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\mu_z(\mathbf X_i)-\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\mu_z(\mathbf X_j)\right|\\
&=
\left|\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\bigl\{\mu_z(\mathbf X_i)-\mu_z(\mathbf X_j)\bigr\}\right|\\
&\le
\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\left|\mu_z(\mathbf X_i)-\mu_z(\mathbf X_j)\right|\\
&\le
\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}L_z\|D_z(\mathbf X_i)-D_z(\mathbf X_j)\|\\
&\le
L_z\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\rho_{n,z}(i)\\
&=
L_z\rho_{n,z}(i)\sum_{j=1}^n \lambda_{i\to j}^{(z),\mathrm{ex}}\\
&=
L_z\rho_{n,z}(i).
\end{aligned}\]
Now multiply by the relevant outer weight, average over focal units, divide by the relevant denominator, and apply Lemma 4.3 with \(a_i\) equal to the outer estimator weight in the relevant cell. That yields root-\(n\) order for the exact bias term in every cell. \(\square\)

\subsection{4.4 Exact survey-weighted matching-frequency moment theory with no theorem-level
equal-share
collapse}\label{exact-survey-weighted matching-frequency-moment-theory-with-no-theorem-level-equal-share-collapse}

This section is the point at which the survey-weighted theory diverges
from the unweighted fixed-\(M\) theory. The matching \emph{geometry} is
still the usual unweighted nearest-neighbor geometry on the
two-dimensional score space. What changes is the \emph{imputation
algebra}: inside each matched set the missing counterfactual is imputed
by an exact within-match survey-weight-normalized weighted average. Therefore the
theorem-level reuse object is not the equal-share count
\[\frac1M\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)\mathbf 1\{j\in\mathcal J_D(i)\},\]
but the exact within-match survey-weight-normalized survey-weighted matching frequency induced by the estimator. The
goal of this section is to separate explicitly:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  what is imported unchanged from the two-dimensional matching geometry,
\item
  what is newly created by the survey-weighted within-match normalization,
\item
  and which additional assumptions are merely sufficient special cases
  rather than theorem-level necessities.
\end{enumerate}

\subsubsection{4.4.1 Generic matching-score
setup}\label{generic-matching-score-setup}

Fix one matched-arm score space \[\mathbf D(\mathbf X)\equiv \mathbf D_D(\mathbf X)\in\mathbb R^2,\] with
matched arm \(z_D\in\{0,1\}\). In the PATE cells the focal unit arm is
\(z_R=1-z_D\). In the PATT cells the matched arm is \(z_D=0\) and the
focal unit arm is \(z_R=1\).

Let the outer weight of the focal unit on this matched-arm score space be denoted by
\[w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X),\] and let the within-match weight assigned to matched unit be denoted by
\[w_D^{(D)}(\mathbf X).\] In the four main cells these two generic objects are:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  retrospective PATE on matched-arm score space \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_z(\mathbf X))\):
  \[w_R^{(D_z)}(\mathbf X)=w_{1-z}(\mathbf X),\qquad w_D^{(D_z)}(\mathbf X)=w_z(\mathbf X);\]
\item
  prospective PATE on matched-arm score space \(\mathbf D_z(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_z(\mathbf X))\):
  \[w_R^{(D_z)}(\mathbf X)=w(\mathbf X),\qquad w_D^{(D_z)}(\mathbf X)=w(\mathbf X);\]
\item
  retrospective PATT on matched-arm score space \(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_0(\mathbf X))\):
  \[w_R^{(D_0)}(\mathbf X)=w_1(\mathbf X),\qquad w_D^{(D_0)}(\mathbf X)=w_0(\mathbf X);\]
\item
  prospective PATT on matched-arm score space \(\mathbf D_0(\mathbf X)=(e(\mathbf X),\Psi_0(\mathbf X))\):
  \[w_R^{(D_0)}(\mathbf X)=w(\mathbf X),\qquad w_D^{(D_0)}(\mathbf X)=w(\mathbf X).\]
\end{enumerate}

For matched unit \(j\in\mathcal I_D\) and focal unit \(i\in\mathcal I_R\),
define the matched unit-inclusion indicator
\[I_{ij,D}\coloneqq \mathbf 1\{j\in\mathcal J_D(i)\},\] and define the
exact within-match survey-weight-normalized matched-unit share
\[\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}
\coloneqq
\frac{w_D^{(D)}(\mathbf X_j)}{\sum_{k\in\mathcal J_D(i)} w_D^{(D)}(\mathbf X_k)}\,I_{ij,D}.\]
The exact survey-weighted matching frequency is therefore \[K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j)
\coloneqq
\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}.\]
This is the theorem-level reuse object that enters the matching-frequency
representation of the estimator.

For the geometry part of the proof, define the count-form inclusion
aggregates \[C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)
\coloneqq
\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i) I_{ij,D},\] \[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)
\coloneqq
\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)^2 I_{ij,D},\] and
\[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)
\coloneqq
\sum_{\substack{i,\ell\in\mathcal I_R\\ i\neq \ell}}
w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_\ell) I_{ij,D}I_{\ell j,D}.\] Observe that
\[\label{eq:count-second-split}
\{C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\}^2=C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)+O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j).\] Also observe
that \[\label{eq:exact-second-split}
\{K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j)\}^2
=
\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)^2\{\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\}^2
+
\sum_{\substack{i,\ell\in\mathcal I_R\\ i\neq \ell}}
w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_\ell)\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\Lambda_{\ell\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}.\]

Define the selected-sample matched-arm score density by
\[P_s\{j\in\mathcal I_D,\ D(\mathbf X_j)\in \mathrm d d\}=f_D^s(d)\,\mathrm d d.\]
Define the first and second focal unit-mark densities by
\[E_s\bigl[w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)\mathbf 1\{i\in\mathcal I_R,\ D(\mathbf X_i)\in \mathrm d d\}\bigr]
=
f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)\,\mathrm d d,\]
\[E_s\bigl[w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)^2\mathbf 1\{i\in\mathcal I_R,\ D(\mathbf X_i)\in \mathrm d d\}\bigr]
=
f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)\,\mathrm d d.\]

\subsubsection{4.4.2 Imported unchanged two-dimensional inclusion
geometry}\label{imported-unchanged-two-dimensional-inclusion-geometry}

The geometric part of the proof is still the fixed-\(M\),
two-dimensional nearest-neighbor geometry of Chen--Han. Because the
matching rule itself is unweighted, that geometry does not change. What
changes is only the algebra that turns inclusion events into estimator
coefficients.

\textbf{Imported Input 4.6} (Weighted equal-share two-dimensional
catchment moments). Assume the matched-arm score support is compact, the matched unit
score density \(f_D^s\) is continuous and bounded away from zero, the
two marked focal unit score measures with densities \(f_{R,D}^{(1)}\) and
\(f_{R,D}^{(2)}\) are absolutely continuous and continuous, and \(M\) is
fixed. Define \[L_{n,D}(j)
\coloneqq
\frac1M\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i) I_{ij,D}.\] Then for
\(f_D^s\)-almost every matched-arm score value \(d\),
\[E_s\bigl[L_{n,D}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)},\] and
\[E_s\bigl[L_{n,D}(j)^2\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
\frac1M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}
+
\frac{\alpha(M,2)}{M^2}
\left\{\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\right\}^2.\]

\textbf{Lemma 4.6A} (Count-form inclusion-geometry moments). Under
Imported Input 4.6, for \(f_D^s\)-almost every matched-arm score value \(d\),
\[E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)},\]
\[E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)},\] and
\[E_s\bigl[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
\alpha(M,2)\left\{\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\right\}^2.\]

\textbf{Proof.} The first identity changes only the definition of
\(C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\): \[\begin{aligned}
C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)
&=
M L_{n,D}(j).
\end{aligned}\] Take conditional expectations given \(D(\mathbf X_j)=d\) and
\(j\in\mathcal I_D\): \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&=
M E_s\bigl[L_{n,D}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}.
\end{aligned}\] This proves the first display.

For the second display, apply the same logic to the equal-share object
built from the squared focal unit marks: \[L_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)
\coloneqq
\frac1M\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)^2 I_{ij,D}
=
\frac1M C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j).\] Imported Input 4.6 with focal unit mark
\(w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X)^2\) yields
\[E_s\bigl[L_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}.\] Multiply both sides by \(M\):
\[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&=
M E_s\bigl[L_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}.
\end{aligned}\] This proves the second display.

For the third display, start from
the count-square identity defining \(O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\) and divide
by \(M^2\): \[\begin{aligned}
L_{n,D}(j)^2
&=
\frac{1}{M^2}\{C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\}^2
=
\frac{1}{M^2}C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)
+
\frac{1}{M^2}O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j).
\end{aligned}\] Take conditional expectations given \(D(\mathbf X_j)=d\) and
\(j\in\mathcal I_D\): \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[L_{n,D}(j)^2\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&=
\frac{1}{M^2}E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
+
\frac{1}{M^2}E_s\bigl[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr].
\end{aligned}\] Rearrange: \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&=
M^2 E_s\bigl[L_{n,D}(j)^2\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
-
E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr].
\end{aligned}\] Apply the imported second-moment limit for
\(L_{n,D}(j)\) and the just-proved limit for \(C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\):
\[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&\to
M^2\left[
\frac1M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}
+
\frac{\alpha(M,2)}{M^2}
\left\{\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\right\}^2
\right]
-
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\\
&=
\alpha(M,2)\left\{\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\right\}^2.
\end{aligned}\] This proves the third display. \(\square\)

\textbf{What is imported unchanged.} Lemma 4.6A does not change the
matching geometry. It is just the imported two-dimensional
inclusion-geometry result rewritten in count form instead of equal-share
form. The estimator has not entered yet.

\subsubsection{4.4.3 Exact matched unit-share factorization
condition}\label{exact-matched unit-share-factorization-assumptions}

The next exact-share condition is the survey-weight object that replaces the
old equal-share bridge. It does \emph{not} assert that the exact
within-match survey-weight-normalized share is close to \(1/M\). It leaves the exact
matched unit-share heterogeneity visible in the main theorem path and only asks
for asymptotic factorization of the exact matched unit-share moments on the
matched-arm score space. Instead it asks that the exact matched unit-share weighted
sums factor asymptotically into:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  an inclusion-geometry part, and
\item
  an exact local matched unit-share moment factor.
\end{enumerate}

\textbf{Definition 4.7} (Finite-\(n\) exact-share moment maps). For \(f_D^s\)-almost every matched-arm score value \(d\) for which the denominators below are positive, define
\[\label{eq:share-factor-1}s_{n,D,1}(d)
:=
\frac{E_s\bigl[K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
     {E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]},\]
\[\label{eq:share-factor-2}s_{n,D,2}(d)
:=
\frac{E_s\bigl[\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)^2\{\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\}^2\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
     {E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]},\]
and
\[\label{eq:share-factor-11}s_{n,D,11}(d)
:=
\frac{E_s\bigl[\sum_{\substack{i,\ell\in\mathcal I_R\\ i\neq \ell}} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_\ell)\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\Lambda_{\ell\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
     {E_s\bigl[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}.\]

\textbf{Closure Input 4.7A} (Convergence of the exact-share moment maps). The three finite-\(n\) maps in Definition 4.7 admit pointwise limits for \(f_D^s\)-almost every \(d\):
\[s_{n,D,1}(d)\to s_{D,1}(d),
\qquad
s_{n,D,2}(d)\to s_{D,2}(d),
\qquad
s_{n,D,11}(d)\to s_{D,11}(d),\]
and the three sequences are dominated by a common integrable envelope of order \(2+\delta\) for some \(\delta>0\).

\textbf{Remark 4.7A.} The present note no longer states this step as a loose ``there exist some functions'' assumption detached from the finite-\(n\) estimator. The primitive objects are the three finite-\(n\) exact-share moment maps in Definition 4.7. Closure Input 4.7A only asks that those specific finite-\(n\) objects converge. This is the exact-share analogue of passing from finite-\(n\) reuse probabilities to their probability limits in the unweighted fixed-\(M\) theory.

\textbf{Remark 4.7B.} Closure Input 4.7A is still an exact-share condition. It does \emph{not} assert that the exact within-match survey-weight-normalized share is close to \(1/M\). The equal-share case is recovered only under an \emph{additional} sufficient condition implying
\[s_{D,1}(d)=\frac1M,
\qquad
s_{D,2}(d)=\frac1{M^2},
\qquad
s_{D,11}(d)=\frac1{M^2}.\]
Those equal-share values are \emph{not} imposed in the main theorem path.

\textbf{Theorem 4.8} (Exact survey-weighted matching-frequency moment theorem on a
two-dimensional matched-arm score space, no theorem-level equal-share
approximation). Assume the matched-arm score space is two-dimensional,
Imported Input 4.6 holds, Lemma 4.6A holds, and Closure Input 4.7A holds.
Then, for \(f_D^s\)-almost every matched-arm score value \(d\),
\[E_s\bigl[K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j)\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
\kappa_{D,1}^{\mathrm{ex}}(d)
\coloneqq
s_{D,1}(d)
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)},\] and
\[E_s\bigl[(K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j))^2\mid D(\mathbf X_j)=d,\ j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
\to
\kappa_{D,2}^{\mathrm{ex}}(d)
\coloneqq
s_{D,2}(d)
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}
+
s_{D,11}(d)
\alpha(M,2)
\left\{\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\right\}^2.\]

\textbf{Proof.} The first moment is immediate from
the definition of \(s_{n,D,1}(d)\) and the first
display of Lemma 4.6A: \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&=
\frac{E_s\bigl[K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
     {E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
\times
E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(1)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]\\
&\to
s_{D,1}(d)
\times
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}.
\end{aligned}\] This proves the first display.

For the second moment, start from the exact identity
the exact second-moment split identity and take
conditional expectations given \(D(\mathbf X_j)=d\) and \(j\in\mathcal I_D\):
\[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[(K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j))^2\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&=
E_s\Bigl[\sum_{i\in\mathcal I_R} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)^2\{\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\}^2\Bigm| d,j\in\mathcal I_D\Bigr]\\
&\quad+
E_s\Bigl[\sum_{\substack{i,\ell\in\mathcal I_R\\ i\neq \ell}} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_\ell)\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\Lambda_{\ell\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\Bigm| d,j\in\mathcal I_D\Bigr].
\end{aligned}\] Apply
the defining ratio for $s_{n,D,2}(d)$ to the first term
and the defining ratio for \(s_{n,D,11}(d)\) to the
second term: \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[(K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j))^2\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&=
\frac{E_s\bigl[\sum_i w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)^2\{\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\}^2\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
     {E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
E_s\bigl[C_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]\\
&\quad+
\frac{E_s\bigl[\sum_{i\neq \ell} w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_i)w_R^{(D)}(\mathbf X_\ell)\Lambda_{i\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\Lambda_{\ell\to j,n}^{(D),\mathrm{ex}}\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
     {E_s\bigl[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]}
E_s\bigl[O_{n,D}^{(2)}(j)\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr].
\end{aligned}\] Now send the first ratio-limit, first count-moment
limit, second ratio-limit, and off-diagonal count-moment limit to their
limits one piece at a time: \[\begin{aligned}
E_s\bigl[(K_{n,D}^{\mathrm{ex}}(j))^2\mid d,j\in\mathcal I_D\bigr]
&\to
s_{D,2}(d)
\times
M\frac{f_{R,D}^{(2)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\\
&\quad+
s_{D,11}(d)
\times
\alpha(M,2)
\left\{\frac{f_{R,D}^{(1)}(d)}{f_D^s(d)}\right\}^2.
\end{aligned}\] This proves the second display. \(\square\)


\textbf{Remark 4.8AA} (Location of the optional equal-share collapse results). The optional equal-share collapse route, including Corollary~4.8A and Lemma~4.8B, is collected in Appendix~P so that the main body of Section~4 remains on the exact-share scale.

\textbf{Proposition 4.8C} (General local conditional matched-side survey-weight-law sufficient route to Closure Input~4.7A). Fix one matched-arm score space \(\mathbf D\) and its matched arm. Suppose that for \(f_D^s\)-almost every score value \(d\) there exists a conditional matched-side survey-weight law \(G_D(\cdot\mid d)\) supported on a compact interval \([\underline w_D,\bar w_D]\subset(0,\infty)\) such that:

\begin{enumerate}
\def\labelenumi{\arabic{enumi}.}
\item
  the map \(d\mapsto G_D(\cdot\mid d)\) is weakly continuous and all bounded continuous moment functionals up to order two are continuous in \(d\);
\item
  if \(i\in\mathcal I_R\) and \(j_1(i),\dots,j_M(i)\) are the matched units in \(\mathcal J_D(i)\), then conditional on \(\mathbf D(\mathbf X_i)=d_i\), on \(i\in\mathcal I_R\), and on the vanishing-radius event \(\rho_{n,D}(i)\to 0\), the vector
  \[
  \bigl(w_D^{(D)}(\mathbf X_{j_1(i)}),\dots,w_D^{(D)}(\mathbf X_{j_M(i)})\bigr)
  \]
  converges jointly in distribution to an exchangeable limit
  \[
  \bigl(W_{D,1}(d_i),\dots,W_{D,M}(d_i)\bigr)
  \]
  with common one-dimensional marginal law \(G_D(\cdot\mid d_i)\);
\item
  bounded convergence applies to the share functionals displayed below.
\end{enumerate}

Define the local normalized-share vector by
\[
\zeta_{D,m}(d)
:=
\frac{W_{D,m}(d)}{\sum_{r=1}^M W_{D,r}(d)},
\qquad m=1,\dots,M,
\]
and define the corresponding local exact-share moment factors by
\[
s_{D,1}^{\mathrm{law}}(d):=E[\zeta_{D,1}(d)],
\qquad
s_{D,2}^{\mathrm{law}}(d):=E[\zeta_{D,1}(d)^2],
\qquad
s_{D,11}^{\mathrm{law}}(d):=E[\zeta_{D,1}(d)\zeta_{D,2}(d)].
\]
Then Closure Input~4.7A holds with
\[
s_{D,1}(d)=s_{D,1}^{\mathrm{law}}(d),
\qquad
s_{D,2}(d)=s_{D,2}^{\mathrm{law}}(d),
\qquad
s_{D,11}(d)=s_{D,11}^{\mathrm{law}}(d).
\]
Moreover, exchangeability and the identity \(\sum_{m=1}^M\zeta_{D,m}(d)=1\) imply
\[
s_{D,1}(d)=\frac1M,
\qquad
M s_{D,2}(d)+M(M-1)s_{D,11}(d)=1.
\]

\textbf{Proof.} Fix one score value \(d\) for which the conditional law exists. Because the matching radius on the two-dimensional score space vanishes, every matched unit in \(\mathcal J_D(i)\) lies in a shrinking score neighborhood of \(d\). By assumption, the matched-side survey-weight vector therefore converges jointly to
\[
\bigl(W_{D,1}(d),\dots,W_{D,M}(d)\bigr).
\]
Apply the continuous mapping theorem to the normalized-share map
\[
g(w_1,\dots,w_M)
=
\left(
\frac{w_1}{\sum_{r=1}^M w_r},
\dots,
\frac{w_M}{\sum_{r=1}^M w_r}
\right),
\]
which is continuous on \([\underline w_D,\bar w_D]^M\subset(0,\infty)^M\). Hence the exact within-match coefficient vector converges jointly in distribution to
\[
\bigl(\zeta_{D,1}(d),\dots,\zeta_{D,M}(d)\bigr).
\]
Because every share lies in \([0,1]\), bounded convergence yields convergence of the three finite-\(n\) exact-share maps in Definition~4.7 to the displayed limits. This proves Closure Input~4.7A.

For the final identities, use exchangeability one coordinate at a time:
\[
E\!\left[\sum_{m=1}^M \zeta_{D,m}(d)\right]
=
